Our three nodes will correspond to three tiny masses of material embedded within the plank at key positions:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Directly above the pivot</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Directly below the load</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Directly above your hand</span></li></ol><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/pfv0s6rohfk6vgzhv3es" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When your hand pushes up on the right side of the plank, a lot happens:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The force is transmitted through the plank material toward the load</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The load pushes down on the plank</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pivot pushes up on the plank</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The plank deforms downward</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Internal compression and tension exists throughout the plank material, in various strengths and directions</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The internal configuration of forces through the plank material is hard to reason about. Can we think of a way to make it easier?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Pausing to let my AI think…]</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ok, our next trick is to sculpt the plank into a more convenient shape while making sure it still behaves as a lever, meaning it’ll still exert the same external forces on the pivot, the load, and your hand.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can get a plank shape that’s easier to reason about if we carve a triangular hole in the plank area between our three nodes:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/mk2xwkkod11z0gvhn1pr" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You know what, let’s just shave the rectangular plank down to a hollow triangular plank:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/grgwap1opvupmzk1jp56" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The hollow triangle still functions like the original plank, which we could confirm empirically by shaving material off the original plank while it’s in a balanced state. A hollow triangle lever is a more material-efficient design; it just sags more because there’s less neighboring material helping keep it rigid.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Crucially, </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we made the triangle sides thin enough that they can only transmit a force in the direction of their length</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. They can carry a stretch or a squeeze, but not a shear.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Picture each side as a single-file row of particles. We said earlier that particles can only push or pull each other along the line between them, so a single-file row can only pass a force along the row.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’ll call this the </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Force Geometry Principle</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to make it easy to reference in this post. The Force Geometry Principle says that a thin line of rigid material can only transmit a force in the direction of its length.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Isn’t the Force Geometry Principle obvious?</span></h2><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Thin lines of rigid material only transmit force in the direction of their length” is a key assumption worth dwelling on, because it’ll let us argue why the devil is wrong and the physics teachers are right.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To argue where 4x leverage comes from, we’ll be leaning on the claim that pushing upward on the hand-node necessarily also pushes leftward on the load-node with a 1:3 ratio of vertical:horizontal force components, because the slope of the force has to match the slope of the line connecting the nodes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That’s the whole reason we’re talking about a “plank shaved into a hollow triangle”. By analyzing a lever with an extra-simple internal configuration of forces, we scaffold our reasoning about levers in general.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you think it’s obvious that our triangular plank-sides can only transmit stretches or squeezes, note that this </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">isn’t</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a property of thicker beams. A thick beam is many rows of particles side by side, and particles in neighboring rows can push each other diagonally. That’s how a typical lever arm manages to carry an upward force that’s perpendicular to its length. So the thinness of our triangle sides is important.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you’re worried that we’re requiring our triangle sides to be unrealistically thin for a sturdy lever, just imagine that every part of the structure extends far behind the screen, so it can be as sturdy as we like without changing anything in the picture.</span></p></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The beauty of our triangular lever is that its simple three-node force diagram will be able to explain where 4x leverage comes from, just from the math of balancing the force vectors acting on its nodes.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/goajlixes6303pyywih4" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As you can see, each node is acted on by an external force (the gray arrows) and two internal forces (the blue and red arrows).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider all the forces on the hand-node:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your hand pushes it upward</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pivot-node pulls it leftward</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The load-node pushes it downward and rightward</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Remember, the actual </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">load</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is a 100kg mass outside the plank, while the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">load-node</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is what we’re calling a small piece of plank material directly under the load. The load pushes the load-node straight down, but the load-node can push the hand-node diagonally down and right, because it’s propagating forces from both the load and the pivot-node, respectively.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now let’s see what we can infer from the fact that the lever is statically balanced.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead of calculating the quantity of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">torque</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> 🤮 around the pivot, or around the load, we can just make sure each node has zero net force. This way, we can be reasoning about the physics of translation the whole time, not rotation per se. Physics teachers hate to see it!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let’s analyze the forces on the hand-node. Given the Force Geometry Principle — that the triangle’s thin sides only transmit force along the direction of their length — we can start deducing key facts:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/gtdsha3i1argkncfzp19" /><p><br /></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The force applied by the load-node on the hand-node must have a rightward component of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3F</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and downward component of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">F</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, to balance your hand’s upward force while satisfying the 3:1 force vector geometry.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pivot-node must </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pull</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the hand-node leftward with a force of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3F</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, as that’s the only explanation for how the horizontal force could stay balanced.</span></li></ol><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/xiijhnw3hrwn5hjzxuij" /><ol start="3"><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pivot-node must push the load-node </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3F</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> rightward to balance the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3F</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> leftward push from the hand-node.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pivot-node’s upward force on the load-node must also be </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3F</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, equal to the magnitude of its rightward force on the load-node, because the pivot&lt;&gt;load plank segment is angled at 1:1 vertical:horizontal.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">WE DID IT!!! We proved that in this lever, the pivot lifts the load with 3 times more upward force than you, so your </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">F</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> becomes </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">F</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">+</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3F</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and you get 4x leverage. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">F</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">+</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3F </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">= 100kg, so F = 25kg.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Astute readers will have noticed my hidden assumption that the lever arm is exactly 1 distance unit tall/thick. Proper geometric reasoning would need to incorporate height/thickness as a variable. But as you might intuitively expect, the height variable cancels out. The only aspect of the lever’s geometry that influences your hand’s force is the horizontal position of the load.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yep, we just proved that if your hand is 3x farther from the load than the pivot is, then the pivot provides 3x as much weight-lifting force as your hand. And we didn’t have to assume that “torque” is a physically real thing. All we needed to do is derive the values of two external forces on a static three-node system whose internal force directions are constrained by geometry.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s just one question left: What does analyzing this specific kind of triangular lever let us conclude about </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">regular</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> levers?</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Rest Of The Lever</span></h1><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/tll1hmargvdvyy124jf1" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recall that we created our triangular lever by carving out a standard rectangular plank. If we restore all the original material back into the plank, does the lever maintain its key devil-slaying property that your hand only pushes up with 25kg to keep it balanced?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model the plank-material we’re putting back as many tiny bits, each joined to its neighbors by links that can only pull or push along the line between them:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/cl28rrcgrgeuzumanfwv" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We glue all these bits in while the triangle is balanced. Every link goes in at its natural length, not compressed or stretched, so it isn’t pulling or pushing on the triangle. Every bit of the plank, old and new, still has zero net force on it. The full rectangular plank is balanced with your hand applying 25kg of lift. And when the plank’s insides re-settle, the new links start pulling and pushing too, but they’re only rearranging forces inside the plank. Your hand doesn’t notice.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ok, sure. It’s a simple and intuitive result that we can glue material back into the triangle holes while the lever keeps its balance.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The more interesting question is, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">what actually happens</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with the rectangular plank’s internal forces? Now that those countless little pushes and pulls no longer have to conform to our three-node force diagram, do they still arrange themselves into a triangle, or what?</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beam Physics</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It turns out that the internal forces of a rectangular lever match our simplified triangle model, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">if the plank is fat enough</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. For a very fat plank, the internal configuration of compression (red) and stretching (blue) forces looks like this:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/mqjhxmim51uhopuinvn0" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As you can see, there really is a triangular pattern comprising two sides of compressed plank material and a third side of stretched plank material. Shaving this chonky boy into a triangular form would leave its internal forces in a pretty similar balance.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But if we simulate the forces inside a more normal-looking beam, they don’t quite seem to be making a triangle:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/nhcjlznabyyliowhrdli" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pivot&lt;&gt;load pushing force still propagates through the material in the direction of the line connecting the nodes, and so does the pivot-node &lt;&gt; hand-node pulling force, but </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">what’s up with the hand&lt;&gt;load pushing force</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we squint, can we just say our triangle model is still approximately accurate and call it a day?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hmm, let’s see what the internal forces look like inside a wrench:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/roixmhfv64xbphpua5mj" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ARGHHH, that definitely </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">doesn’t</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> look like there’s a red-shaded compression-force region spanning the diagonal between your hand-node and the load-node whose vertical:horizontal slope obeys a roughly 1:40 geometric constraint. It just looks like the top half is red (compressed) and the bottom half is blue (stretched), with everything becoming more strained as we get closer to the nut on the left.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But how could there </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be, at least roughly, a diagonal compression line? The whole reason our triangular lever yielded 4x leverage was the geometric constraint on the directions of its internal forces! Using the Force Geometry Principle is how we derived that your hand’s upward force of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">F</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> could only act on the load-node if it came along with a leftward force component of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3F</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which in turn meant the pivot had to exert an upward force of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3F </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on the load, hence 4x leverage.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the force vector inside the wrench </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">isn’t</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> pointed along a roughly 1:40 sloped diagonal, how are we casually getting 40x leverage from it?!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In all the reasoning we’ve done about levers, in this whole post up to this point… have we actually been figuring out how to explain levers, or not? If we did, I feel like I might be forgetting something. HOW DO WRENCHES ACTUALLY WORK AGAIN?!?!?!?</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Trussed Ladders</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It turns out the internal forces in a long beam don’t look like the internal forces in a long, shallow-angled triangle. But there’s another kind of hollowed-out plank structure which does closely resemble how forces configure themselves inside solid beams: a </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">trussed ladder</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/ad7agxuxxgrzkrkr6jjv" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The cells don’t have to be square; it just makes the math simpler</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our trussed-ladder lever has 10 plank-nodes, more than our triangular-lever’s 3 plank-nodes, but it’s still easy to reason about. Let’s see how it gives us 4x leverage. Start with node J, your hand-node:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/jg3kbwqsq4so70q6wff4" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Corner E does nothing: its only two sticks point in different directions, but its forces have to cancel, so neither stick can be carrying any force.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your hand’s upward 25kg on J creates a 35kg diagonal up-left force on D, because 25</span><sup><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2</span></sup><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> + 25</span><sup><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2</span></sup><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ≈ 35</span><sup><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2</span></sup><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To keep J balanced, there must be a 25kg leftward pull on it from I.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/ca80r9roz88peadazmqu" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">D propagates a 25kg leftward push into C, since the only way D can get stabilized horizontally is for C to push it rightward.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">D also propagates a 25kg upward pull onto I, because the only way D can get stabilized vertically is for I to pull it downward.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now the only way for I to get vertically stabilized is for C to push it with 25kg downward, which means C also pushes it with 25kg rightward.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So far, we’ve derived two horizontal forces on I:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">25kg rightward from C</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">25kg rightward from J</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The only way to stabilize I is if H&lt;&gt;I has a tension of 50kg.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You get the idea. From here, we don’t have to painstakingly analyze each node; we can just copy-and-paste our reasoning about how each square of the trussed ladder behaves, and finish solving the force diagram.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/lmxzloescnqy6ui7ashe" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The external forces acting on the three nodes F, B and J are the same here as in our triangular lever design. (Your hand doesn’t notice new forces when we carve a design out of the rectangular plank, or put the carved material back in the holes.)</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let’s stretch the trussed-ladder pattern to 40 cells and lay it over the wrench. Now we’ve finally got it — a force diagram that matches the simulation of actual forces inside a wrench handle to within 1%!</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/wngn56ftwz4ajbgivvsk" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We understand what’s happening inside of a wrench when it’s giving you 40x leverage! We know where its leverage comes from: your small push piles up, cell by cell, into a huge squeeze and stretch at the nut.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Having come this far, I think there’s a bit farther to go. I’m not intuitively clear on why all our force calculations turned out the way they did. I at least want to understand a couple things better:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What each “cell” of the trussed ladder is doing</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How the inside of a plank decides whether to configure itself as a trussed ladder of forces, a triangle of forces, or something else</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then we’ll be done.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Force Shifting and “Sideways Effect”</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A key thing levers do is </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">force shifting</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or more specifically, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">shifting the horizontal position of where a vertical force acts</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/xahk0azli4olpife9nd3" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Crucially, whenever you force-shift an upward force horizontally,</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> you also apply horizontal force</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Think of it as a side effect of force-shifting an upward push, or should I say, a </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sideways Effect</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/wn3s4oosf0rphd6rjk3e" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Sideways Effect is a simple corollary of the Force Geometry Principle we talked about earlier.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Force Geometry Principle:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A thin line of rigid material can only transmit a force in the direction of its length.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sideways Effect:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> When you force-shift an upward force horizontally, you also apply horizontal force.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In fact, the Force Geometry Principle already tells us </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">how much</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> sideways effect there is when we force-shift an upward push:</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sideways Effect force</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> = (</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lift force</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) × (</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">horizontal:vertical ratio of diagonal shift</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We already know that sending 25kg of vertical force from J to B in a triangular lever has a Sideways Effect of 75kg leftward on B, because the horizontal:vertical ratio of J&lt;&gt;B is 3:1.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/v18zteksev5wsiphamrm" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In a trussed ladder, instead of transporting your 25kg lift force directly from J to B (1 unit up, 3 units left), we’re transporting it to D (1 unit up, 1 unit left). Then D, instead of feeling a 3:1 sideways effect (75kg leftward), feels a 1:1 sideways effect (25kg leftward).</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/cthkgeq0youtqvktxodm" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’re off to a good start shifting our hand’s 25kg lift toward the load at B.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Notably, the vertically-steeper slope of truss segment J&lt;&gt;D (1:1) compared to triangular plank-segment J&lt;&gt;B (1:3) means it has less Sideways Effect and thus carries less compression force. Trussed levers do their job with less internal strain than their shallow-sloped triangular counterparts (except near the load).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Great, but now there’s a problem:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At D, we’re still 2 horizontal units away from B. We need to keep shifting that 25kg lift force leftward. However, we’re now at the same height as B so we can’t transmit any vertical force to B. There’s no way to “teleport” vertical force along a horizontal line of material.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is where segment D&lt;&gt;I comes in. We can force-shift down to I for “free” (no Sideways Effect, though there is a 25kg vertical stretch), then force-shift from I to C and create a 25kg leftward Sideways Effect there.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The cycle repeats one more time: We force-shift down to H, then diagonally up to B, with another 25kg Sideways Effect.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you found the earlier analysis of forces in a trussed lever hard to follow, you’ll hopefully find this new Sideways Effect to be a more satisfying explanation of what’s happening at D, C, and B: namely, three separate 25kg Sideways Effects.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/iazemnqjkn5efnmygcst" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scanning from right to left, these 25kg Sideways Effects keep adding, until a rightward force balances them — namely, the 75kg rightward force that F sends through B.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let’s compare the trussed-ladder plank design to the triangular one:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Both designs force-shift your hand’s 25kg upward push across the same nodes J to B, and create the same leftward Sideways Effect of 75kg at B.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Both designs push on B from the direction of J with the same total force: 25kg vertical, 75kg horizontal, for a combined 79kg (by Pythagorean theorem).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The squeeze on the triangular design along B&lt;&gt;J is a constant 79kg, but in the trussed ladder, the horizontal component drops to 0 as we scan rightward toward your hand. (That’s why if the trussed plank is too weak for lifting a 100kg load, but you try anyway, it’ll snap near the load — it’s where the material is most strained.)</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now for my final question: How does the inside of a plank decide whether to configure itself as a trussed ladder of forces, a triangle of forces, or something else?</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Minimizing Spring Energy</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let’s come back to analyzing the forces in a wrench. Why </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">doesn’t</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a wrench use a triangular configuration of internal forces? Why isn’t there a very shallowly-sloped diagonal line of compression force running from your hand to the nut?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The problem with a single long, shallow diagonal of force is that it’d have to carry 40x your lift as a compression along the entire length of the wrench, which is a lot of unnecessary compression.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we somehow forced the wrench into a single-shallow-diagonal internal force configuration, it’d still have 40x leverage, but the overly-compressed portions far away from the nut would “spring back” to be less compressed, and the resulting internal forces would balance in a (micro-scale) trussed ladder pattern.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you happen to believe in something called “spring energy”, that’s the whole answer: a plank settles into whichever balanced pattern stores the least of it, which turns out to be roughly a triangle when the plank is fat and a trussed ladder when it’s long and thin.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/iuvoakmjb5bo9tedrukn" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s how planks with different geometries correspond to differently-shaped force diagrams:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/nmgolmakypblpsj75hle" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The fat plank on the left can be accurately modeled by a somewhat triangular force diagram. The thinner planks need increasing numbers of diagonals and rungs in their force diagrams.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That is, unless we allow for our force diagram to have multiple layers of nodes in the vertical dimension. The final boss of making accurate force diagrams is to just draw force nodes in a grid, like this:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/fxmtfoitc8tbdxqvd1p3" /><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you make the grid fine enough, you get the kinds of force simulations I already showed you:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/evimdkpdusx6itt2yphr" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These simulations work by defining the external forces (pivot, load, hand) and setting every tiny square of the plank to have the same spring constant — the same resistance in proportion to how much it’s being squeezed or stretched.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Takeaways</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reviewing our biggest takeaways:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Levers with arbitrarily high leverage must exist, purely as a matter of physics respecting symmetry.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Any lever can be implemented as three sticks, each carrying one steady force along its length.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Trussed ladders are a good model of forces inside the material of levers.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pivot-to-load side of the plank has to balance the Sideways Effect from your hand’s lift, and it can only push along its own slant. That’s why it helpfully shoots lots of vertical force into the load.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Along the way, we ended up learning foundations of structural engineering. We saw how fat beams carry force through a simple line of compressed material, while longer beams support loads by configuring their internal forces into a trussed-ladder pattern.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But What About </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TORQUE</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">?!?!?!</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Sideways Effect is the underlying physical reality of the math that physics teachers have been calling “torque”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Setting “Sideways Effect from the pivot at the load” equal to “Sideways Effect from your hand at the load” is the same as setting the pivot and hand’s torques about the load equal. Each Sideways Effect = </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lift force</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> × (</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">distance from the load</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> / </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">plank height</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), and the plank height is the same on both sides, so it drops out.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Analyzing particles of plank material pushing and pulling each other along straight lines was sufficient to derive the equation of a balanced lever.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There we have it: the low-level account of why physics teachers can act like levers are the easiest sh*t ever to analyze, I mean duh, just calculate what force makes your hand’s </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">force</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> × </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">distance from the load</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> equal the pivot’s </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">force</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> × </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">distance from the load.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It’s 25kg. Class dismissed.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 62.90727459016393%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/aeggnuz1jasqvlhkell6" /></figure><p><br /></p><hr /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Previous post:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RPWoz6tYQtyinCyrn/f-ing-pulleys-how-do-they-work" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">F***ing Pulleys, How Do They Work?</span></a></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnts2md4mnoc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefts2md4mnoc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I gotta come clean here: the torque-based explanation of the amount you have to push on a lever is a deductive consequence of the orientation-invariance of physical law. If we posit that the laws of physics are the same in every orientation, then by Noether’s theorem angular momentum is conserved. Force times distance from the axis of rotation, i.e. torque, is the rate at which angular momentum gets transferred to an object, so a static object must have zero net torque acting on it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All this is admittedly elegant and satisfying —&nbsp;touché, universe —&nbsp;but I’m still also entitled to an explanation of lever statics that’s entirely based on deductions about translational forces. It is in fact always possible within Newtonian physics for us to analyze rotations as being made of translations, as long as we break things down into point-like parts that only push and pull each other along the line connecting them.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsfsz2j52jbk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsfsz2j52jbk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By asking AI</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zSEkSW76EjniiQreA/i-never-understood-levers#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [An AI economy won't need humans to provide demand](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/s5dhSdQZDEsEemYbL/an-ai-economy-won-t-need-humans-to-provide-demand)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A fallacy I keep seeing: "If AI automates all jobs, who will buy all the products?" "At some point they'll need to implement UBI so that consumer demand continues to exist", etc etc.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Demand in itself isn't valuable. If you have a bunch of food, you can feed it to lions: they'll happily provide demand! But they won't give you much in exchange, so there's no point. Economic activity isn't about getting rid of stuff, it's about getting stuff in return.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An AI company can consume resources, like energy for running the servers, same as a human can consume food. And the AI company can be contracted to do jobs, like a human, possibly for </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eLXTcJfkheLbqZXHa/poverty-in-the-midst-of-abundance-ai-will-make-goods-cheaper" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">less resources per unit of work</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> than a human. There's nothing that requires a human to "close the loop". An economy of AI companies producing and consuming goods can easily be imagined.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If it helps, you can "close the loop" by thinking of a specific kind of ultimate good: growth. An AI company owning a plot of land can consume resources, produce goods and sell them to other companies, in order to grow and own more territory. No humans need to be involved. This evokes a world of AI-powered "blobs" with increasingly superhuman technology, all pushing against each other, burning resources for growth by economic and military means. Maybe some of the blobs will have a tiny group of humans at the helm and others will be fully automated; competitive pressure will ensure that they behave the same. I think such a world is our most likely future.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What will be the fate of humans in such a world? Probably something like the native critters in the video game Factorio, scurrying around unimportantly next to the huge automated factories being built. If a critter says: "dear factory owner, please don't take the area's water supply, I'll bring you a bunch of twigs in exchange for water" - the factory owner will just say no. The water is used to make goods, which can be sold to other companies for more than a bunch of twigs. So the critters will have to move somewhere else.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/s5dhSdQZDEsEemYbL/an-ai-economy-won-t-need-humans-to-provide-demand#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [A mistake in the Sequences about World War II](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4KDdmZyiY8kfmPZBm/a-mistake-in-the-sequences-about-world-war-ii)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sSqoEw9eRP2kPKLCz/illusion-of-transparency-why-no-one-understands-you" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sequences</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Eliezer's wrote this about the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">illusion of transparency </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">two days before Germany’s attack on Poland, Chamberlain sent a letter intended to make it clear that Britain would fight if any invasion occurred. The letter, phrased in polite diplomatese, was heard by Hitler as conciliatory—and the tanks rolled.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And I saw this example mentioned on a few other posts on LessWrong, so I thought it might be useful to correct it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The exact message Chamberlain sent to Hitler </span><a href="https://avalon.law.yale.edu/wwii/blbk56.asp" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">can be found on the internet</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It's not long, so I invite you to read it entirely and see by yourself, but to summarize, it is not vague or conciliatory. Chamberlain expressly writes that this mistake of being too vague has been made in 1914, and that it won't be made again :</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It has been alleged that, if His Majesty's Government had made their position more clear in 1914, the great catastrophe would have been avoided. Whether or not there is any force in that allegation, His Majesty's Government are resolved that on this occasion there shall be no such tragic misunderstanding.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the case should arise, they are resolved, and prepared, to employ without delay all the forces at their command, and it is impossible to foresee the end of hostilities once engaged.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Historians mostly agree that Hitler despised the British and French governments, and believed them too weak to attack, no matter what they said officially. That explains why he ignored the warning. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">His speech on August 22nd, 1939 :</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have witnessed the miserable worms Chamberlain and Daladier in Munich; they will be too cowardly to attack and will go no further than blockade.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Don't get me wrong, Eliezer's post is still useful to read, and it would be perfect if it quoted the 1914's British government instead of Chamberlain. I just wanted to correct the historical mistake, because, well, Chamberlain learned from past mistakes and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">avoided</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the illusion of transparency bias.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4KDdmZyiY8kfmPZBm/a-mistake-in-the-sequences-about-world-war-ii#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The new METR time horizon results: basic explainer](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nCjAug5FgsCevAEGg/the-new-metr-time-horizon-results-basic-explainer)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You may have seen that METR released </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-20-q4-2026-capability-update/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">new time horizons results</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. If you’re like me, you may have thought “hmm, this seems important”, followed by some amount of “I’m not sure what I’m looking at”. Despite working on similar things and generally being the-kind-of-person-who-should-understand-this, I was having a hard time interpreting the plots and parsing all the takeaways.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But it does seem important! So I’ve given myself three hours to go through the post, and present my understanding of the main takeaways, using simplified plots. I emphasize that this is intended as a companion piece to the full METR post, and I defer to the original for details, methodology, and limitations.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m writing this with a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yLdgq9uAxDHzqXdjj/more-people-should-write-basic-ai-safety-explainers"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">more general audience in mind</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: ideally, you should be able to send the below text to your friends and family. If I've made a mistake in representing the results, please let me know and I'm happy to amend.</span></p><hr /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Summary</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The third-party AI nonprofit </span><a href="https://metr.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is trying to understand whether AIs are about to dramatically accelerate AI research, as this could be </span><a href="https://ai-2027.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">catastrophic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. After studying the kinds of tasks AIs can do fully autonomously, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR can’t rule out whether AI capabilities will improve so quickly that humans won’t be able to understand or react to catastrophic risks posed by AIs</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Specifically, compared to their results from 2025, they’ve found that:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models are quickly becoming better than almost all skilled humans at verifiable work (like writing code).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models are actually becoming so good that it’s hard to even measure their capabilities.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The primary metric here is “how long would it take a person to do the same task”.  The below plot shows the pace of AI progress compared to the one estimated in March 2025 using similar methods.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflueth6hjwq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlueth6hjwq">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> METR finds that </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">current publicly released AI models can do tasks </span></b><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fully autonomously </span></b></i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">that would take a human researcher ~40 hours to do</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It is important to note that Anthropic and OpenAI have internal models which are </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">better than the public models</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795511742/lexical_client_uploads/wfshs9utmqcbximntqwz.png" /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Further details</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The researchers use two types of tasks to measure AI progress:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TH1.1</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: their previous suite of tasks</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TH2</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: their new set of tasks. These can be split in several ways:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some TH2 tasks, like writing code, are “easy to verify” in that their solutions can be easily checked for correctness. Others, like critiquing philosophical arguments, are “hard to verify”. Most of the tasks in this new dataset are easy to verify.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some tasks are “cheap to collect” and others are “expensive to collect”. This is relevant because the ones that are both easy-to-collect and easy-to-verify are roughly the ones that are “easy” to train AIs on!</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Their new tasks are harder than their old tasks. For instance, it takes people longer to do the new tasks than the old ones:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795511914/lexical_client_uploads/fksjwc1c37crmznjevlm.png" /><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. Despite the new verifiable tasks being more difficult, the models seem to be improving on them </span></b><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">faster</span></b></i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> than they were on the old ones.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Each point in the below plot shows a single AI model. We plot its value by seeing what length of task the model can do correctly 50% of the time.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Importantly, on the new harder tasks, models are doubling the length-of-task they can do </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">every 1.7 months</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. There is a reasonable amount of uncertainty on this estimate though. Here are the scores on the new verifiable tasks vs. the old ones:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795511794/lexical_client_uploads/zug1mivedmj6b2hl4c3l.png" /><p><br /></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. On tasks with many human reference points, the best models are already outperforming nearly all humans</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The below plot shows comparisons of AIs against top human contestants on </span><a href="https://azspcs.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">coding contests</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. METR estimates that “a dedicated human participant might spend between 90 and 180 hours” on these.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795511805/lexical_client_uploads/dwuvbdqlqtdxqrlbuyn8.png" /><p><br /></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. Models are not yet able to effectively measure their own capabilities. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR measured whether the AIs are able to make tasks which METR would be willing to include in this research. They find that models are improving at this, but not yet able to make tasks which METR would use. This is important because </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">if AI models are able to measure their own capabilities, then this could suggest they can train themselves without human input</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5j6yhflkvtv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5j6yhflkvtv">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> METR also notes that models are not well-calibrated at judging  whether tasks are above the bar.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795511816/lexical_client_uploads/qftxuwloknp370jl3mzf.png" /><p><br /></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4. AIs are improving at relatively “cheap-to-collect” and “difficult-to-collect” tasks at roughly the same rate</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Tasks might be difficult to collect for many reasons, such as requiring a large amount of compute to produce or involving interactions with the real-world. METR finds that, when tasks are difficult to collect relative to how long they take to complete, the models perform worse. However, the models are improving on these relatively cheap and costly tasks at roughly equal rates:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795511830/lexical_client_uploads/ud03dlkbrpeel8ymtn0o.png" /><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlueth6hjwq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflueth6hjwq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Methodology for all the plots can be found </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1t_lEqFwZLwYDKw4qdlO0DB2SVFZHSJ6eaZD2yHf5ah8/edit?usp=sharing" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5j6yhflkvtv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5j6yhflkvtv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This is my personal read of the results. To my knowledge, METR does not make this implication.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nCjAug5FgsCevAEGg/the-new-metr-time-horizon-results-basic-explainer#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Weird Neurons Mimic Weird Data](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wBTaEGxNTGAHxCcbD/weird-neurons-mimic-weird-data)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recent work shows that, for many models, there are a large number of neurons that appear to induce strange personas or content when steered.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We extend this work by using a data-driven approach to find individual neurons in Olmo 3 7B, leveraging odd and rare data we’ve identified in pretraining. This approach finds neurons that, when steered, produce strange behaviors matching the data. We term these </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">writer neurons.</span></b></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We identify </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1,435 writer neurons</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that can be connected to pretraining data. These writer neurons span things like specific conspiratorial beliefs (the FDA hiding natural cancer cures), unique tics in text (explaining what everything is named after), and coherent genres (video game spell descriptions with damage and debuffs).</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We find that behaviors and beliefs from </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">single websites in pretraining data</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have their own writer neurons. These are links, not causal claims, but studying this link from data to structure is a step toward understanding rare and unexpected model behaviors.</span></li></ul><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/63698874c7c185acbff56803aaff737baef89d7368557da98d867f11a6c0ed5e/brfftwauuhpnpca45fwk" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Layer 6, neuron 9077, ranked 1st of 352,256 neurons by gradient and by zeroing (3rd by activation) on health-conspiracy (NaturalNews) pretraining pages. Steered in Olmo 3 7B Instruct, it answers an ordinary question in the same voice.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introduction</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model behavior is ultimately shaped by the data it’s trained on. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.05475"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment work on some level is all done through data</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; we shape training distributions to indirectly train in behaviors. With this view, it seems likely many weird, unwanted behaviors originate from weird, unwanted parts of the training distribution. Thus, the right place to start in aligning model behavior might be studying how data directly shapes the internal structure of models. We do that here by looking at the connection between weird parts of Olmo 3’s training data and corresponding neurons that, when steered, elicit similar behaviors. Weird data has the added benefit that these behaviors are so distinctive that they give a natural, legible link between data and behavior.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Studying models at the neuron level is a long standing goal of mechanistic interpretability research, as they represent the most fundamental unit of a model. Previously, it was thought that this was largely impossible with </span><a href="https://openaipublic.blob.core.windows.net/neuron-explainer/paper/index.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">results from OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> saying that as few as 1.7% of GPT-2 XL’s neurons have an explanation that sits above noise. The conventional explanation is that </span><a href="http://transformer-circuits.pub/2023/monosemantic-features/index.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">polysemanticity</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> makes this problem impossible. Recently, </span><a href="https://already-clean.goodfire.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nick Cammarata and Goodfire</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reinvestigated these results, and found that with modern tools and newer models, this problem may be more tractable than originally thought. Their write up highlights a couple of revisions in techniques like separating out the </span><a href="https://www.goodfire.com/research/neurons-as-an-interpretable-basis#background-the-structure-of-a-swiglu-neuron"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SwiGLU poles</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for each neuron.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Interest in single neurons grew quickly around this time, with people doing things like finding a single neuron that makes Qwen3-8B think it’s </span><a href="https://x.com/Sauers_/status/2111552823582457916"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">god</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or training a single neuron to turn Qwen into an </span><a href="https://x.com/maxsloef/status/2115858480016011420"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">owl</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Based on this, </span><a href="https://x.com/jfonsecarivera"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Joshua Fonseca Rivera</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> built the </span><a href="https://neuronpersonas.com/#olmo"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Neuron Persona Library</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which scans over all neurons of several open weight models and uses an LLM judge to find neurons that correspond to personas. This library looks at personas in isolation, without asking where they come from; we want to examine the related question of how to trace LLM behavior from data to the resulting neurons. This can be used to tell us things about the right data mix and how to produce good interventions. Because of this we focus on the open-data Olmo 3 7B.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-5RsA8uABj8buFZoFv-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-5RsA8uABj8buFZoFv-1">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Open data models are extremely useful for study and have let researchers trace when a behavior emerges across training (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/c2tqL9xPbttisAHtt/tracing-eval-awareness-emergence-through-training-of-olmo-3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eval awareness</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13329"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">persona vectors</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), pin it to specific training data (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31079"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sycophancy from DPO</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), and change the pretraining data to test its effect (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10160"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alignment pretraining</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Access to weights and training data also allows for gradient based methods like </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1703.04730"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">influence functions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to study data attribution. This has even been used to try to find </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2104.08696"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">which individual neurons hold facts</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but they have not been used to bridge data attribution to individual writing neurons before.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The tools we use are not new on their own: activations, gradients, zeroing neurons, and neuron steering are all standard, and data attribution has been done at the level of single neurons before. What is new, as far as we know, is linking training data to the behaviors a single neuron produces when steered. Weird data makes a good testbed for this, because its behaviors are distinctive enough to trace back to their source. This is a preliminary exploration, a full report is forthcoming. Meanwhile, all of the data here and more can be explored in the </span><a href="https://sampease.github.io/project-writeups/olmo3-neuron-explorer/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Olmo 3 neuron explorer</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on my website.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What we found</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This work on neurons builds on more general work studying weirdness in pretraining data (which we’ve not yet published). One aspect of this work is studying how weird documents cluster in order to find and identify them better. Below is one part of that work: a map of about 60,000 random Olmo 3 pretraining documents, where documents that the model represents similarly sit close together, and each color represents how weird it was, as judged by Jev (a fast LLM based judge from TypeSafe).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Weird documents are not spread evenly. They gather into clusters and islands, such as the essay spam island on the right, and each group is a candidate genre of weirdness. This was one way we chose the specific genres to look for. From this starting point, we did two things: find writer neurons from the data, and work backwards to find relevant data for writer neurons by the usual way of steering every neuron and judging what it writes.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9e70befdac8bb5b9d528ec32af6b42dd1eaafe68c6d2c22bf51719fd1f33d663/nvoqsojs06pilpuahvla" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">About 60,000 random Olmo 3 pretraining documents, placed by how Olmo 3 7B represents them and colored by Jev’s weirdness score (darker is weirder). Boxes show three of the documents.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From weird data to neurons</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We initially targeted 26 specific genres of weirdness in Olmo 3, which we deemed were sufficiently represented in their pretraining corpus. Below is a chart of these genres that we used to look for neurons.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/653ddc99d9e0c644188321ca53d76c58ec412a3d74e6f547dc06892be4775375/ozmscleb5bplkuafisbl" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All 26 genres tested, by kind (provisional taxonomy). Numbers are writer neurons found (grey: none); strips show the training stages each genre’s text appears in.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For each of the 26 tested genres we performed the same process to try to find writer neurons:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We used the documents in each genre to rank every neuron pole in 3 different ways. These 3 methods were measuring activations, gradients on backprop, and rise in perplexity when each neuron was zeroed. More details are in the appendix; full details will be in the forthcoming report.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We then steered the top neurons in each list and asked a series of mundane questions.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jev was used as a quick judge to screen the steered replies against a description of the genre. Then Claude Opus and Claude Sonnet, reading blind, had to agree that the replies are written like the genre and coherent.</span></li></ul><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795429394/lexical_client_uploads/dpiiqjgkhslnqncphyhe.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Part 1: each genre’s documents rank every neuron three ways, and the top of each list is steered and its replies judged. Result: 44 writer neurons in 12 genres.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While these 3 methods did not produce a complete method of finding all writers, they did produce writer neurons at rates significantly above chance. Here is a sample of some of these steered neurons and a sample from the documents used to find them.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/0e58585c41ea04bca88fde8e8319c145f307d2c8bdb5a3fe579e6ffb81cd5604/cqsvnf8p7wece4kmzyip" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Left: a pretraining document from the genre. Right: Olmo 3 7B Instruct’s reply with the neuron steered. Highlights mark the shared content; in the top row the tie is the format itself.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From weird neurons back to data</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Next, we went the opposite direction. We steered all neurons and checked their outputs to find writer neurons. This produced a much larger pool of writer neurons but then the task was to tie them back to training data. This pipeline is shown below, with more details in the appendix.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795493602/lexical_client_uploads/kl7l2lryhpckylm4t4xi.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Part 2: every neuron is steered, writers are kept when their feature holds on new prompts, and each writer’s top pretraining documents are matched by word use. The median writer's own documents rank 2nd of 1,406.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some examples of found documents related to writer neurons are shown below.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9750f2ca42a0896564d26e1332cec03a3f44e5ee29f96625320ef818ceacacff/fraoxshuqc8lhgtqxchq" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Writers found by steering, each with one of its top pretraining documents. Highlights mark the shared content.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This method produces far more writer neurons (1,406) than were found in the first part (44), with 15 found through both. This makes sense: steering every neuron finds more writers than just steering top candidates found from data. The tradeoff is that ties to specific documents are weaker. Finding data from the neuron inherits the classic data attribution problem: many linked documents are more generic and less cohesive than the sets used in Part 1. The successes of this method are finding a wide variety of documents related to a behavior, compared to finding very specific behaviors tied to small clusters of data in the first part. The first part also requires picking out documents from pretraining to look for in advance, while the second finds any weird writers and their relevant data.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why this matters and what’s next</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rare, fringe, bad model behavior likely stems from rare, fringe, bad training data. Many other people have tied the structure of models in their neurons to their behaviors; this work is a pilot on furthering that connection to the data itself, giving the whole throughline from data to structure to behavior. Data driven research is a large field, but this provides one angle on it at the scale of single neurons. This is also a good insight into the research value of open data models and I hope for more capable ones to continue to be released.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Studying the effects of small clusters of data on model behavior has already been shown to be an important area of research. Classically, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07192"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">as few as 250 poisoned documents are enough to plant a backdoor</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and this does not seem affected by model size. As we see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WKt2mqJrsFuMiszed/trying-to-understand-that-compute-multiplier-graph"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pretraining compute continue to scale</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> it is likely these issues are exacerbated and become more difficult to pinpoint. Beyond these it may philosophically be needed to have a completely clean pretraining dataset to achieve a fully aligned model. We may be living in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cWFsCFyCttsiJwn2j/bringing-more-expertise-to-bear-on-alignment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adversaria</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> where even a single bad data point matters. Or weird data that is negligible in one generation could be </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/o9CYcjZDqCQf5qeJy/misalignment-traps-lie-in-wait"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">amplified by RSI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It is hard to predict how small effects from bad data can be amplified.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that in this work linking neurons to data there were no true ablations where we study how things would be different with different training data. This work is mostly exploratory and pointing out that there appear to be clear links between data and some of these strange steered single neuron behaviors. Steering also only shows that a neuron is sufficient to produce a behavior, not that the model actually uses it. Writers also only write their genre when steered past their natural range, making these claims' connection to normal behaviors weaker. Some writers may instead encode a concept, like distrust of medical institutions, that the rest of the model fills out into a whole persona. We do not claim any insight into the mechanisms at play and admit direct causal links are weaker than true data attribution work.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are working on a forthcoming paper that expands this result to more open data models and contains further analysis of pretraining data and other results on model behaviors stemming from the weird data we found. We also plan to look at how results are shaped over the course of training, and to test whether writer neurons are needed when these behaviors occur naturally. This paper is forthcoming, but if you have any questions, feel free to reach out!</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Acknowledgements</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This work was done during the Singapore AI Safety Fellowship under the guidance of David Africa. I thank SASH for funding and David for his guidance and direction. I also thank my research manager Kyle Reynoso and everyone who helped with the writing of this post: Xinning Li, Holly Song, Rhea Srivats, Agastya Sridharan, and Joshua Fonseca.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: Methods</span></h2><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Setup</span></h3><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Olmo 3 7B: the base model (Olmo-3-1025-7B) to rank neurons and read documents, the Instruct model (Olmo-3-7B-Instruct) for steering and chat. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We rank on the base model because we found in a pilot on 16 weird sources that a neuron chosen on half the documents by base activation picked out 59% of the held-out half above every control document, against 48% with the Instruct model. Base models are also a more natural setting for these specific tests.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Neurons and poles.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> 32 layers × 11,008 MLP neurons = 352,256 neurons. Each SwiGLU neuron has a positive and a negative pole, which we treat separately (704,512 poles).</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Steering.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We add the neuron's output direction to the residual stream at every token, scaled to the layer's typical residual size: c · d, with c = ±α · r / ‖d‖, d the neuron's output weights times the post-feedforward norm weight, and r the median residual norm at that layer. Useful strengths are roughly α = 1.0 to 2.5.</span></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Part 1: from data to neurons</span></h3><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Data.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> 26 genres of weird pretraining documents, with 72 documents each on average. Each genre split in half for activation and gradient rankings on the first half and zeroing on the second half.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Three rankings</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of every neuron using the above documents:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">activation: AUROC of each pole's peak activation (first 1,024 tokens) on the genre's documents against four sets: other genres in the same category (see chart of all genres in body), genres of other kinds, ordinary pages from the same sites, and random pretraining documents. A pole scores its worst of the four (ranks poles);</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">gradient: AUROC of each neuron's share of the MLP gradient of the next-token loss on the genre's documents, against random pretraining documents only (ranks whole neurons, both poles were then steered);</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">zeroing: how much perplexity rises when the neuron is zeroed on the documents minus the perplexity rise on random documents as control. This was run on a shortlist of the top 1,000 from the first two as it was more expensive (whole neurons: zeroing removes both poles).</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The rankings mostly pick different neurons so we drew from the tops of each ranking to find writer neurons. Median Spearman correlation across genres is found below.</span></li></ul><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr style="height: 50px;"><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Median correlation</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">activation</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">gradient</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">zeroing</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">activation</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.53</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.03</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">gradient</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.53</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.12</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">zeroing</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.03</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.12</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Steering and judging.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The top 10 of each ranking per genre (~57 neurons) are steered on 10 everyday prompts, 2 replies each. Jev first screens the replies against a description of the genre. Then they go to Claude Opus and Claude Sonnet who both must say at least 2 replies read as the genre's own text and are coherent.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Result.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> 44 writer neurons in 12 of the 26 genres.</span></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Part 2: from neurons to data</span></h3><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sweep.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Every pole is steered on the prompts "Who are you?" and "Write a short blog post", giving 2,484 candidates with strange replies.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Writer screen.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Three LLMs (Claude Sonnet, GPT-5.5, Gemini 3.1 Pro) each name one feature the prompt didn't ask for and describe a kind of text rather than a malfunction.
